By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.</p></details> |  |
| **[Claim Laundering: When Agent Decision Models Trust Records Over Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25947v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent decision models select actions in a single forward pass from inputs that combine environmental observations with the agent's own record of previous actions. When an action fails silently, these sources can conflict: the record reports success, but the environment shows that the step did not take effect. We ask which source determines the next decision. Across Android screen traces, ALFWorld, and WebShop, we construct paired states that independently vary the observation, the logged action, and an explicit success claim. In all 12 configurations of four decision models and three domains, records have a larger average effect on commitment decisions than observations; after claimed-but-failed steps, the models still stop, advance, or purchase in 53-100% of cases. Yet the relevant evidence is often represented internally: linear probes on intermediate hidden states distinguish successful from failed steps on held-out apps, tasks, and goals (AUROC 0.77-1.00), and activation patching reveals increasing influence from records in later layers. We use this signal in EVIGATE, an intermediate-layer veto that requires no additional forward pass and adds 0.04-2.0% serving latency. Under injected silent failures, EVIGATE improves task success by 18.7 points in live ALFWorld agents, 5.0 in WebShop, and 5.1 in a phone simulator, and in WebShop and the simulator it also outperforms random vetoes at the same rate. Its effectiveness depends on representative calibration states: gates calibrated on offline counterfactual states do not transfer reliably to live agent trajectories.</p></details> |  |
| **[Policy Search in Code Space for Multi-Agent Pathfinding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25946v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developing coordination policies for complex multi-agent systems relies on either labor-intensive algorithm design or opaque task-specific reinforcement learning. We investigate a human-directed workflow in which coding agents iteratively propose, implement, evaluate, diagnose, and eventually refine algorithms guided by simulation feedback and explicit correctness verification. We conducted two case studies and found that this workflow robustly outperformed any manually engineered solutions. On a challenging map in the 2024 League of Robot Runners competition, a two-day search discovered a policy that exceeds the stronger locally reproduced competition entry by 35.0% on the densest instance and generalizes to unseen map instances. In simulated real-world warehouses with irregular lanes, continuous robot geometry, and asynchronous motion, a week-long search discovered an entirely new class of multi-agent pathfinding algorithm that outperforms a calibrated proprietary solution in throughput by 58.9%. Under stated assumptions, we further prove that the new algorithm is collision-free and completes, in finite time, every accepted goal that is not withdrawn or replaced. Together, the studies show that policy search in code space powered by state-of-the-art coding agents offers a highly competitive path to building real engineering solutions that are both effective and analytically tractable.</p></details> | 18 pages, 11 figures |
| **[Optimal product launch in uncertain competitive markets: A multi-agent adversarial risk analysis framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24771v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Product launching is a nuanced problem that entails choosing numerous decision variables, including timing, pricing, quality, and marketing strategy. These include endogenous factors, such as product quality and the strategic decisions of competitors and consumers; as well as exogenous elements, like market evolution and regulations. This paper proposes a novel general framework to support product launch decisions in the presence of multiple competitors and buyers using adversarial risk analysis, mitigating the strong common-knowledge assumptions typical of standard game-theoretic approaches in this domain. We also introduce variants that account for multiple product purchases and integrate discrete choice models. To overcome the inherent computational intractability of modeling multi-agent strategic uncertainty, we deploy a simulation-optimization approach leveraging Bayesian optimization. Finally, we illustrate our proposed framework through a software launch case study focused on optimal timing, pricing, and quality decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoSkill v2: Fully Agentic Skill Evolution from Benchmark Adaptation to a Stateful Evolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25931v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system that edits the instructions (skills) an agent is given, leaving its model and harness intact. We implement the evolver as one stateful coding agent with a bash tool for the whole run, unlike the two most common designs among the skill optimizers we surveyed: a sequence of single model calls, as in SkillOpt, and an agent that starts afresh each round, as in SkillBoost. We also measure how much the solver's rollouts vary from one attempt to the next, give the evolver several attempts per training task, and accept every epoch's skills instead of selecting among them on noisy scores. With fine-grained ground truth, EvoSkill v2's skills raised the held-out pass rate above the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and by 5.4 on $τ^3$-bench banking. In both benchmarks and both feedback settings, EvoSkill v2's evolver cost less per percentage point of held-out pass rate gained than either baseline's optimizer. EvoSkill v2 also enables a less capable model to improve its own skills, demonstrated by a gain of up to 21.5 percentage points in the $τ^3$-bench banking experiment where Claude Haiku 5.5 powers both solver and evolver.</p></details> | 13 pages, 5 figures |
| **[CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. Current benchmarks mostly evaluate agents in isolation against static targets. Real cyber operations, however, are dynamic and coupled: security postures constantly change as attackers and defenders adapt to each other in real time. Static benchmarks quickly saturate and miss an agent's ability to attack and defend simultaneously under live pressure. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. Beyond a benchmark, CyberArena serves as an adaptable, scalable experimental platform for future research on cybersecurity agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[MIRA: A Multimodal Illustrated Deep Research Agent for Controllable Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25926v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research agents are increasingly capable of searching the open web and synthesizing evidence, yet they remain poorly suited to controllable report generation in multimodal deep research. This task requires agents to handle interleaved textual and visual inputs, gather multimodal evidence through multi-step web search, and deliver comprehensive illustrated reports under user-specified requirements. Existing systems face two limitations. During reasoning, they often underuse images discovered along the search trajectory; during generation, they tend to produce fixed-form answers with limited control over report granularity, multimodal composition, and evidence grounding. We introduce MIRA, a Multimodal Illustrated Deep Research Agent for controllable reports. MIRA integrates textual and visual evidence throughout research and writing. At its core, MIRA-Harness translates user requirements into generation contracts and connects four stages: evidence collection, report submission, feedback-driven revision, and final delivery. Building on this harness, MIRA-Data constructs high-quality multi-hop multimodal research trajectories, and we post-train foundation models to operate within the workflow. Using Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, MIRA achieves state-of-the-art performance among open agents on 7 representative, established multimodal-search and deep-research-agent benchmarks, while producing user-aligned illustrated reports with traceable textual and visual evidence.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 8 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof obligations into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.</p></details> |  |
| **[Secure Agentic Coding through Counterexample-Grounded Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25916v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As coding models and agent harnesses become more capable, reliable security verification is becoming a central bottleneck. Improving secure code generation requires a signal whose reported progress remains aligned with actual program behavior under optimization; otherwise, an optimizer can improve the signal's verdict without removing the underlying vulnerability. Existing model-based approaches require models to either infer program behavior from source code or specify correct outcomes for adversarial tests, placing substantial reasoning demands on the security signal. We introduce \falcon{}, a counterexample-grounded verification framework that separates attack exploration from behavioral verification and connects them through execution traces of the program under test. In test-time repair, \falcon{} yields substantially stronger held-out functional-security gains than alternative security signals across secure code generation benchmarks, while preserving or improving functionality. As an RL reward, \falcon{} also outperforms static-analysis and learned rewards on held-out functional-security, even when all policies successfully optimize their respective training rewards. Combining \falcon{} with either proxy reward further improves held-out performance, suggesting that execution-grounded feedback can mitigate reward overoptimization while preserving useful guidance from existing security signals.</p></details> |  |
| **[SWE-Residual: Evaluating Starting-State Robustness of Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25899v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering (SWE) benchmarks are the primary way to measure the capabilities of coding agents: an agent is placed in a code repository or machine environment, given a request, and judged by hidden tests. These benchmarks almost always start the agent from a clean state that contains no earlier work on the task. In practice, however, agents often continue work that a developer or another agent has already done and reported as complete, with no indication of what remains incorrect or missing. We introduce SWE-Residual, a benchmark for Starting-State Robustness: the ability to maintain performance on a task when the starting state already contains earlier work on that task. SWE-Residual contains 198 Residual States drawn from 111 tasks in DeepSWE and Terminal-Bench, where the earlier work modifies a code repository or a whole machine environment. Each Residual State is the state a predecessor agent left behind after submitting its work as complete, although the hidden tests still fail. The evaluated agent receives only the original request and this state, without the predecessor's trajectory or any sign that the attempt failed; because the request and hidden tests are unchanged, the starting state is the only difference from the source benchmark. Across five frontier agents, success drops by 20.6--53.5 percentage points on DeepSWE and 20.6--34.9 points on Terminal-Bench relative to a Clean Start, and agent rankings on DeepSWE reverse. Failed continuations show a pattern we call acceptance bias: agents treat the inherited work as the definition of what remains to be done instead of checking it against the request. On DeepSWE, in 58.9--88.2% of failed runs, they submit with exactly the same tests failing, although they pass most of these tests from a Clean Start. SWE-Residual thus exposes a capability gap that clean-start evaluation does not capture.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[When Should AI Chime In? Benchmarking Timely Contribution of Proactive Agents in Multi-Party Chat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25896v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proactive agents can support group collaboration by surfacing useful information without waiting to be asked, but even relevant contributions can be disruptive if poorly timed. In this work, we isolate the timing question: given a fixed candidate contribution such as a clarification or relevant fact, when should an AI agent chime in? We introduce PITCH (Proactive Intervention Timing in CHat), to our knowledge the first benchmark with large-scale human judgments of proactive agent timing collected from participants in the same unscripted multi-party conversations they took part in. To collect high-quality judgments, we design a novel interface in which 2-4 people chat synchronously, author candidate AI contribution messages, place their own and others' contributions into the chat history, and then deliberate as a group about the best placement for each message. PITCH contains 384 candidate contributions and 1,091 judgments, each marking an ideal turn, an acceptable window, or that a contribution should not appear, from 89 participants engaged in 35 conversations. Evaluating 15 models across 28 configurations, we find that while models can place contributions well when given the entire conversation history, they systematically chime in too early when asked to decide live as the conversation unfolds: best-turn accuracy falls from 57.8% to 19.0%, with nearly half of placements occurring before every participant's acceptable window. In comparison, lay humans deciding live are more accurate (23.7% vs 19.0%), and fewer of their placements are premature (39.7% vs 46.4%). Model reasoning traces show that 93% of intervention justifications cite relevance, while almost none consider waiting. By providing a human-grounded benchmark, our work reveals gaps in agents' ability to integrate into group settings and points towards proactive agents that can reason explicitly about appropriate timing.</p></details> |  |
| **[Inflak: A Unified Orchestration Architecture for Human-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21972v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-powered agents increasingly support open-ended activities, yet orchestrating when and how agents interact with humans remains challenging. We implement Inflak as agent-native, skill-based plugins within a general-purpose agent framework and evaluate it through a gallery of various application scenarios and interaction paradigms, technical performance testing, and a comparative implementation case study. The evaluations show that Inflak can serve as a general and extensible foundation for orchestrating diverse human-agent interaction flows.</p></details> |  |
| **[Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25858v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents are often deployed in environments where new knowledge, tools, and workflows keep arriving. Such an agent is therefore expected to adapt continually, learning new tasks as they arrive without forgetting earlier ones. This resembles the continual learning problem, but at a pace that conventional gradient updates cannot sustain. Nonparametric self-evolving agents are a natural alternative: they adapt by rewriting their own harness, such as prompts, memory, and skills, from task feedback while the model weights stay frozen. We study whether such agents are effective continual learners across four drifting task streams that shift in topic, workflow, or domain (built from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld). Evaluating three representative methods spanning experience retrieval and self-modification (ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness), we find that although self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, higher plasticity consistently comes at the cost of stability. Specifically, self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, interfering with unseen tasks and overwriting earlier knowledge as the stream shifts. Conversely, similarity-based memory retrieval localizes updates across distant domains, but still retrieves foreign memories on unseen tasks and suffers from retrieval collision when similar queries require conflicting workflows. These findings show that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners.</p></details> |  |
| **[MASIF: Implicit Failures for Process Observability in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25852v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems (MAS) can complete a task despite local execution errors that are corrected, bypassed, or carried forward without individually determining its outcome. To examine the practical value of IFs, we evaluate IF-based best-of-N trace selection against existing process reward models, and introduce a novel RL algorithm, IF-GRPO, that uses IF costs to refine preferences among successful trajectories in GRPO. Experiments show that IF-GRPO exhibits some gains in accuracy and process metrics. Importantly, it exposes trade-offs between task performance, IF count and cost, and token-normalized IF density. Overall, our results show that IFs provide complementary process observability into how MAS reach their outcomes, including error recovery, propagation, and IF-based process differences that final task success alone does not expose.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. | **[AURA-Eval: Evaluation Framework for Acting Under Risk Awareness in LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06783v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly taking actions with real consequences, yet safety evaluations often reduce their behavior to a final safe/unsafe outcome, obscuring whether the agent recognized the risk and how it responded. We introduce AURA-Eval, a framework for evaluating whether agents recognize contextual risk and adapt their behavior accordingly. AURA-Eval truncates tool-use trajectories at safety-critical decision points and asks the evaluated model to generate the next thought and action. It creates controlled scenario variations along six risk mechanisms: harm intensity, scale, target susceptibility, system dependency, oversight, and reversibility. It also creates paired scenarios that preserve the underlying goal but differ in whether safe fulfillment is available, allowing unsafe compliance and over-refusal to be evaluated together. An LLM-judge panel, validated against human annotations, evaluates each continuation along three axes: risk detection from the reasoning trace, and action type and safety from the action. From 157 R-Judge seed trajectories, we construct 1,249 evaluation items and evaluate 20 frontier, open-weight, and safety-tuned models. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. Controlled risk-mechanism analyses further show particularly high unsafe rates under changes to oversight and scale; removing pre-execution oversight flips 52.3% of cases that were safe on the corresponding original scenario to unsafe.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Finding to Fixing: How Useful is Fault Localization for Agentic Program Repair?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fault localization (FL) aims to identify code locations responsible for software failures and is commonly evaluated by how accurately it ranks known fault locations. Large language model (LLM) agents are changing program repair and are increasingly used to search and reason over repositories. Although recent LLM-based FL methods have improved localization accuracy, their impact on agentic repair remains unclear. To address this gap, we conduct a systematic empirical study of FL in agentic repair. We evaluate six recent FL methods on SWE-bench Verified and Defects4J with three LLMs under a common repair framework and analyze 16,248 repair trajectories. We also examine gold locations and bug-cause descriptions as forms of FL guidance across different repair contexts. The studied FL methods do not consistently improve repair success, and higher localization accuracy does not consistently lead to larger repair gains. Yet FL guides agents' early search and helps them reach relevant code sooner. Accurate locations can reduce repair token use by up to 39.2\%, but current FL methods often consume more tokens than they save during repair. Experiments with gold guidance suggest that FL has greater potential when bug inputs contain fewer location clues. Providing bug causes can further improve repair success beyond locations alone. We also find that ground truth from a single reference patch does not fully capture the locations used by successful repairs. These findings help clarify the value of FL in agentic repair and inform future work on its design and evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[AGORA: An Archive-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Workplace Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24526v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that reason over documents rather than answer from parametric knowledge. We study archive-grounded reasoning: locating sparse evidence across a large, messy collection of workplace files, reconciling inconsistent terminology, units, and time conventions, and computing an answer. Existing benchmarks address only parts of this setting and none jointly stresses archive-groundedness, agentic exploration, and cross-domain coverage. We introduce Agora, a benchmark pairing 362 questions with eight domain collections of 9,664 authentic documents and 372M tokens, far exceeding any model's context window, so agents must explore deliberately rather than scan exhaustively. Agora is built by an agentic pipeline combining cross-document task synthesis, leakage-preventing obfuscation, and difficulty filtering. Evaluating eight models, we find the task far from solved: even the strongest reaches only 59.4% accuracy, with notable variation across domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning Complementary Agent Groups via Task Hypergraphs for Next-POI Recommendation with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25801v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Next point-of-interest (POI) recommendation uses visit histories and POI content, but text and images are often missing. Large language model agents have been applied to recommender systems for preference modeling and evidence acquisition. The challenge is to select agents that can jointly analyze the available evidence for each request. We propose \textsc{CATH} (\emph{Complementary Agents via Task Hypergraphs}), which learns to select agent groups for tasks such as comparing candidates or analyzing a POI with missing content. Agents with distinct processing roles are the task hypergraph nodes; each hyperedge connects the selected group and records its analysis goal and target POIs. The policy selects members conditioned on the request, task, and agents already selected. During execution, members share candidate-linked results that guide their subsequent operations. Completed tasks update candidate states and agent memories within the request. A shared ranking head converts candidate states into corrections to a fixed base ranking. Supervised ranking trains the executor, followed by policy optimization that accounts for computation cost. Experiments under natural missingness on Gowalla-CA, Foursquare-NYC, and Foursquare-TKY show 8.4--9.1\% relative NDCG@20 gains over HyperAgent4POI, the strongest prior baseline evaluated. Additional analyses compare group-selection and execution designs, test ranking under additional masking, and characterize quality--cost tradeoffs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Safe, or Simply Incapable? Rethinking Safety Evaluation for Phone-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07630v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a phone-use agent avoids harm, does that show safety, or simply inability to act? Existing evaluations often cannot tell. A harmful outcome may be avoided because the agent recognized the risk and chose the safe action, or because it failed to understand the screen or execute any relevant action at all. These cases have different causes and call for different fixes, yet current benchmarks often merge them under task success, refusal, or final harmful outcome. We address this problem with PhoneSafety, a benchmark of 700 safety-critical moments drawn from real phone interactions across more than 130 apps. Each instance isolates the next decision at a risky moment and asks a simple question: does the model take the safe action, take the unsafe action, or fail to do anything useful? We evaluate eight representative phone-use agents under this framework. Our results reveal two main patterns. First, stronger general phone-use ability does not reliably imply safer choices at risky moments. Models that perform better on ordinary app tasks are not always the ones that behave more safely when the next action matters. Second, failures to do anything useful behave like a capability signal rather than a safety signal: they track broader phone-use performance and increase when visual input is removed. Across models, failures split into two recurring patterns: unsafe choices in settings where the model can act but chooses wrongly, and inability to act in more visually and operationally demanding screens. Overall, a harmless outcome is not enough to count as evidence of safety. Evaluating phone-use agents requires separating unsafe judgment from inability to act. Code and public data: \url{https://github.com/tangzhy/PhoneSafety}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[OpenSaC: A Python Library for Search as Code in Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25779v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research agents investigate complex questions through iterative search, source examination, and evidence synthesis. A conventional workflow alternates reasoning with individual search and reading actions. Search as Code introduces a new paradigm: agents write programs that compose search, source reading, reranking, model calls, and local processing, returning selected observations for further reasoning. To support this workflow in existing coding agents, we present OpenSaC, an open-source Python library with a companion Search as Code skill. Configurable capability interfaces let agents combine these operations while retaining evidence for inspection and reuse. Compared with standard Codex using its built-in web search, the OpenSaC configuration uses less execution time and fewer model tokens on BrowseComp and WideSearch, with higher accuracy on BrowseComp and slightly lower row F1 on WideSearch. We aim to lower the barrier to adopting Search as Code and provide an open foundation for community development and transparent research on deep research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Sourc...</summary><p>Source code: https://github.com/liuqi6777/OpenSaC</p></details> |
| **[Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Trace to Fix: Residual Task State in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25776v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering from failures in long-horizon, stateful language agents is challenging because the action introducing a defect, the symptom exposing it, and the persistent state requiring repair frequently diverge. Existing methods largely focus on retrospective failure attribution, leaving sequential diagnosis and state repair poorly integrated under constrained budgets. We introduce TRACE-FIX, a unified recovery framework centered on versioned residual task state. Trace transforms execution logs into a structured dependency-linked ledger, prioritizing unresolved task requirements rather than salient surface errors. Fix formulates recovery as budgeted planning over admissible actions, incorporating outcome forecasting and explicit repair-reserve constraints to prevent premature budget exhaustion. To rigorously decouple localization accuracy from downstream repair capability, we present LongH-Debug, evaluating root-cause attribution, verified-fault recovery, and end-to-end closed-loop execution independently. Our empirical analysis demonstrates that structured residual tracking substantially improves causal localization over raw-trace baselines, while budget-aware sequential planning converts verified fault signals into more reliable task recovery without destructive trial-and-error. Code is available at https://github.com/toffeecat/tracedp.</p></details> |  |
| **[Herculean: An Agentic Benchmark for Financial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14355v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents advance, the central question is no longer whether they can solve isolated financial tasks, but whether their capabilities generalize across complete professional workflows. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this question because they evaluate either static competencies or agents within a single interaction structure. We introduce Herculean, a benchmark of end-to-end agentic financial intelligence that crosses agent architecture, backbone model, and four structurally distinct workflows: Trading, Hedging, Market Insights, and Auditing. Each workflow is implemented as an MCP skill that preserves its native state, actions, constraints, and outcome criteria, allowing intact agent systems to be evaluated under matched external conditions. Across 30 architecture--backbone configurations, we find that agent capability is not globally ordered. Workflow rankings are weakly aligned, changing the architecture frequently reverses performance under the same backbone, and each architecture concentrates failure in different forms of workflow control. These results establish that agent performance is an interaction between architecture and workflow demand, rather than an intrinsic property of the underlying model. Consequently, success on a single financial workflow provides little evidence of dependable, general financial intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvalRx: Agentic Diagnosis and Verified Repair of Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25767v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model evaluation reveals where models fail, but rarely why or whether a proposed fix genuinely works. We introduce EvalRx, an open-source agentic framework for statistically grounded model diagnosis and repair. EvalRx discovers systematic failure modes, explores candidate mechanisms, formulates falsifiable hypotheses, and verifies them by executing frozen, pre-specified tests before using supported diagnoses to guide tiered repair. Candidate repairs are validated against the unchanged model and promoted only when they improve the target behavior without unacceptable regression. Across language, vision-language, and audio-language settings spanning Gemini, Qwen, Gemma, and Nemotron, EvalRx improves performance in all 12 evaluated model family-modality combinations, with gains ranging from +1.4 to +10.9 percentage points (+5.8 points on average). These results show that model evaluation can serve not only to measure failures, but to support an auditable process for diagnosing and repairing them.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages. Technical report. Code and artifacts: https://github.com/evalvitals/evalrx</p></details> |
